Does that sound like the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did something very upsetting or traumatic happen to you around the time of the merger at work?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not traumatic in the way I’d understand that. It just sort of… threw everything off, I guess. Is that what you mean, or are you asking about something more serious?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this lack of useful sleep?\n\nPatient: It’s been about three years, I’d say… since the merger and when they reorganized our Caribbean-market division. Yes… I think so. I don’t like admitting that, because I keep telling myself I’m still functioning, but it’s definitely affected things.

At work, my models and presentations have been slipping enough that I actually got a formal performance warning. That was… really embarrassing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess... mostly sleep, or the lack of it. I mean, I’m still functioning at work, so I don’t want to make it sound overly dramatic, but I’m barely sleeping in a way that feels useful anymore. I’ll lie there replaying client models in my head, and then I wake up around 3 or 4 with my stomach all tight. By the time I get to work, it feels like I never really rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think anxious is probably the right word. I don’t want to overstate it, but when I wake up around 3 or 4, my stomach is really tight, and my mind goes straight back to work—client models, numbers, things I might’ve missed. Then I just sort of lie there, and by morning it feels like I haven’t really slept in any useful way.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical complaints like headaches or muscle tension apart from the tight stomach you mentioned?\n\nPatient: Not really, I don’t think. I mean, nothing that feels worth making a big deal about. Mostly I notice the stomach tightness. I haven’t really been tracking headaches or muscle tension, so… I’m not sure if that’s helpful.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain things or situations because of your anxiety or stress?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding things exactly… well, maybe I am. I’ve stopped going to a few things I used to be really consistent with—volunteer teaching, yoga, book club. I keep telling myself it’s just because work is busy, and I don’t want to seem flaky, but I just can’t make myself show up lately.\n\nAnd at work… this is embarrassing, but I did get a formal performance warning. My models and presentations have been slipping. I’m trying to keep it together, but I guess the stress is making me pull back from everything else. Does that count as avoidance?\n\nDoctor: Is the anxiety you experience mostly about work or does it also involve other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Mostly work, I think. That’s where my mind goes first—client models, numbers, things I might’ve missed. I’ll lie awake replaying it, and then if I wake up at 3 or 4, it’s like I’m already back at my desk in my head, with my stomach tight.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but… I came in because I barely feel like I sleep in any useful way anymore. I go to work feeling like I never actually rested. Mostly work, honestly. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m obsessing in some dramatic way, but when I’m lying there, I’ll replay client models or presentations in my head and think about what I missed or what could go wrong.

It’s mostly work-related, yes. I don’t think there’s much else that I’m lying there worrying about in the same way.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess... mostly sleep, or the lack of it. I mean, I’m still functioning at work, so I don’t want to make it sound overly dramatic, but I’m barely sleeping in a way that feels useful anymore. I’ll lie there replaying client models in my head, and then I wake up around 3 or 4 with my stomach all tight. By the time I get to work, it feels like I never really rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think anxious is probably the right word. I don’t want to overstate it, but when I wake up around 3 or 4, my stomach is really tight, and my mind goes straight back to work—client models, numbers, things I might’ve missed. Then I just sort of lie there, and by morning it feels like I haven’t really slept in any useful way.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical complaints like headaches or muscle tension apart from the tight stomach you mentioned?\n\nPatient: Not really, I don’t think. I mean, nothing that feels worth making a big deal about. Mostly I notice the stomach tightness. I haven’t really been tracking headaches or muscle tension, so… I’m not sure if that’s helpful.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain things or situations because of your anxiety or stress?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding things exactly… well, maybe I am. I’ve stopped going to a few things I used to be really consistent with—volunteer teaching, yoga, book club. I keep telling myself it’s just because work is busy, and I don’t want to seem flaky, but I just can’t make myself show up lately.\n\nAnd at work… this is embarrassing, but I did get a formal performance warning. My models and presentations have been slipping. I’m trying to keep it together, but I guess the stress is making me pull back from everything else. Does that count as avoidance?\n\nDoctor: Is the anxiety you experience mostly about work or does it also involve other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Mostly work, I think. That’s where my mind goes first—client models, numbers, things I might’ve missed. I’ll lie awake replaying it, and then if I wake up at 3 or 4, it’s like I’m already back at my desk in my head, with my stomach tight.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but… I came in because I barely feel like I sleep in any useful way anymore. I go to work feeling like I never actually rested. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Has something specific happened recently that you think might be contributing to your anxiety and sleep issues?\n\nPatient: I guess the clearest thing was the merger at work… when they reorganized our Caribbean-market division. Does that sound like the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did something very upsetting or traumatic happen to you around the time of the merger at work?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I know that’s a long time—I probably should’ve said it more plainly earlier.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of at least a few months where you didn't feel anxious or stressed about work or other areas of your life since the merger?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I’ve had better stretches here and there, so I don’t want to make it sound like it’s been constant panic or anything. But I don’t think there’s been a few months where it truly wasn’t there in the background.\n\nIt’s been going on about three years, since that merger and the Caribbean-market division reorganization. That’s probably the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant distress or impairment in social, occupational, or other important areas of functioning due to your anxiety and sleep issues?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. By the time I go to work, it feels like I didn’t really rest. Then I go to work feeling like I never actually rested.\n\nSo yes, mostly work. Is that specific enough?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about being unable to control your anxiety or is it mostly the work-related issues that are causing you distress?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate those, honestly. I think of it as the work issues causing the anxiety, more than me being afraid of the anxiety itself.\n\nThat said… when it starts going, I don’t feel very good at shutting it off. I don’t know if that counts as “unable to control it,” but it can feel like my mind just keeps returning to the same things. Mostly work things. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Has something specific happened recently that you think might be contributing to your anxiety and sleep issues?\n\nPatient: I guess the clearest thing was the merger at work… when they reorganized our Caribbean-market division. I don’t want to overstate it, because I know everyone had to adjust, but that’s when this really started.\n\nIt’s been… about three years now, I think. Does that sound like the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did something very upsetting or traumatic happen to you around the time of the merger at work?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not traumatic in the way I’d understand that. The merger was stressful and unsettling, but there wasn’t some single awful event that happened to me around then.\n\nI keep wondering if I’m making too much of a work change, honestly. It just sort of… threw everything off, I guess. Is that what you mean, or are you asking about something more serious?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this lack of useful sleep?\n\nPatient: It’s been about three years, I’d say… since the merger and when they reorganized our Caribbean-market division. That’s when I first noticed the sleep stopped feeling useful. I know that’s a long time—I probably should’ve said it more plainly earlier.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of at least a few months where you didn't feel anxious or stressed about work or other areas of your life since the merger?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I’ve had better stretches here and there, so I don’t want to make it sound like it’s been constant panic or anything. But I don’t think there’s been a few months where it truly wasn’t there in the background.\n\nIt’s been going on about three years, since that merger and the Caribbean-market division reorganization. That’s probably the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant distress or impairment in social, occupational, or other important areas of functioning due to your anxiety and sleep issues?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t like admitting that, because I keep telling myself I’m still functioning, but it’s definitely affected things.\n\nAt work, my models and presentations have been slipping enough that I actually got a formal performance warning. That was… really embarrassing. And outside of work, I’ve pretty much stopped going to the things that used to keep me grounded—volunteer teaching, yoga, book club. I keep meaning to go back, but I don’t show up.\n\nSo yes, I guess it has impaired things more than I’d like to say. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your anxiety and sleep issues been present for most days over the past three years?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. It started around the merger, when our Caribbean-market division got reorganized, and it’s been about three years since then.\n\nI wish I could say it was more occasional, but… no, most days is probably accurate.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything besides work that you find yourself worrying about when you're lying awake at night?\n\nPatient: Mostly work, honestly. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m obsessing in some dramatic way, but when I’m lying there, I’ll replay client models or presentations in my head and think about what I missed or what could go wrong.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I’ll wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach tight, already thinking about it again. By the time I go to work, it feels like I didn’t really rest. Is that answering what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there things outside of work that you worry about when you're lying awake, or is it mostly work-related concerns?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly work-related, yes. I don’t think there’s much else that I’m lying there worrying about in the same way.\n\nI mean… the reason I came in is that I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I’ll be awake replaying client models or things I need to present, and then if I do fall asleep, I wake up at 3 or 4 with my stomach tight, already back in it. I don’t know if that counts as “unable to control it,” but it can feel like my mind just keeps returning to the same things. Mostly work things. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there times when you feel anxious and it's not related to work or your job performance?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any clear way. I mean, everyone gets anxious about random things sometimes, I guess, but the times I’m really noticing it and can’t settle, it seems tied to work or how I’m doing there.\n\nIf there are other times, I may not be recognizing them. Does that answer it the way you’re asking?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m obsessing in some dramatic way, but when I’m lying there, I’ll replay client models or presentations in my head and think about what I missed or what could go wrong.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I’ll wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach tight, already thinking about it again. By the time I go to work, it feels like I didn’t really rest. Does that answer it the way you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are you ever anxious when you're not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed. I’m trying to think of an example, and I keep coming back to work things, or worrying about whether I handled something right.\n\nI suppose it’s possible there’s some background tension I’m not labeling that way, but if you’re asking whether I’m anxious just sitting at home with no work thought attached… I don’t think so. It just sort of… threw everything off, I guess. Is that what you mean, or are you asking about something more serious?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this lack of useful sleep?\n\nPatient: It’s been about three years, I’d say… since the merger and when they reorganized our Caribbean-market division. That’s when I first noticed the sleep stopped feeling useful. I know that’s a long time—I probably should’ve said it more plainly earlier.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of at least a few months where you didn't feel anxious or stressed about work or other areas of your life since the merger?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I’ve had better stretches here and there, so I don’t want to make it sound like it’s been constant panic or anything. But I don’t think there’s been a few months where it truly wasn’t there in the background.\n\nIt’s been going on about three years, since that merger and the Caribbean-market division reorganization. That’s probably the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant distress or impairment in social, occupational, or other important areas of functioning due to your anxiety and sleep issues?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t like admitting that, because I keep telling myself I’m still functioning, but it’s definitely affected things.\n\nAt work, my models and presentations have been slipping enough that I actually got a formal performance warning. That was… really embarrassing. And outside of work, I’ve pretty much stopped going to the things that used to keep me grounded—volunteer teaching, yoga, book club. I keep meaning to go back, but I don’t show up.\n\nSo yes, I guess it has impaired things more than I’d like to say. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your anxiety and sleep issues been present for most days over the past three years?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. Maybe I’d phrase it as it’s been there most days, just in varying degrees. It started around the merger, when our Caribbean-market division got reorganized, and it’s been about three years since then.\n\nI wish I could say it was more occasional, but… no, most days is probably accurate.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything besides work that you find yourself worrying about when you're lying awake at night?\n\nPatient: Mostly work, honestly. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m obsessing in some dramatic way, but when I’m lying there, I’ll replay client models or presentations in my head and think about what I missed or what could go wrong.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I’ll wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach tight, already thinking about it again. By the time I go to work, it feels like I didn’t really rest. Is that answering what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there things outside of work that you worry about when you're lying awake, or is it mostly work-related concerns?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly work-related, yes. I don’t think there’s much else that I’m lying there worrying about in the same way.\n\nI mean… the reason I came in is that I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I’ll be awake replaying client models or things I need to present, and then if I do fall asleep, I wake up at 3 or 4 with my stomach tight, already back in it. Then I go to work feeling like I never actually rested.\n\nSo yes, mostly work. Is that specific enough?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about being unable to control your anxiety or is it mostly the work-related issues that are causing you distress?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate those, honestly. I think of it as the work issues causing the anxiety, more than me being afraid of the anxiety itself.\n\nThat said… when it starts going, I don’t feel very good at shutting it off. I don’t know if that counts as “unable to control it,” but it can feel like my mind just keeps returning to the same things. Mostly work things. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there times when you feel anxious and it's not related to work or your job performance?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any clear way. I mean, everyone gets anxious about random things sometimes, I guess, but the times I’m really noticing it and can’t settle, it seems tied to work or how I’m doing there.\n\nIf there are other times, I may not be recognizing them. Does that answer it the way you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are you ever anxious when you're not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed. I’m trying to think of an example, and I keep coming back to work things, or worrying about whether I handled something right.\n\nI suppose it’s possible there’s some background tension I’m not labeling that way, but if you’re asking whether I’m anxious just sitting at home with no work thought attached… I don’t think so. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease significantly if your work situation improved or the sources of stress at work were resolved?\n\nPatient: I think it probably would, yes… at least a lot of it. I don’t want to overstate it, because maybe I’m not seeing the whole picture, but it does feel like work is the main thing setting it off.\n\nIf the pressure or the things I’m worried about there were handled, I imagine I’d feel noticeably calmer. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would completely go away if the work issues that are causing you stress were fully resolved?\n\nPatient: Completely? I’m not sure I can say that. I’d like to think it would, because work really does seem like the main trigger, but I don’t know if there’d still be some leftover tension or worry that I’m not noticing right now.\n\nSo… I think it would probably decrease a lot, but I’m hesitant to say it would disappear completely. Is that a reasonable way to answer?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety started immediately after the work merger, or was there a delay before it began?\n\nPatient: I think it was pretty much tied to the merger. Maybe not like the exact same day, but around when they reorganized our Caribbean-market division, that’s when it really started.\n\nIt’s been going on for about three years now, since all of that changed. I hope that answers what you mean by timing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would be significantly reduced if you were able to leave your current job or the stressful work situation behind?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I mean, I would hope it would help a lot, because that situation feels like such a big part of what keeps me keyed up.\n\nBut I don’t know if leaving would make it all just disappear. Part of me worries I might carry some of it with me, if that makes sense. So I’d say… yes, probably significantly reduced, but I’m hesitant to promise it would solve everything. Does that answer it the way you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
